Prabhupāda wanted us to come to Vṛndāvana for inspiration, and then go back and serve the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement in different parts of the world.”

So there are many “ordinary” devotees who have very solid realizations about Prabhupāda and their place in ISKCON. In the name of gaining higher realizations, I shouldn’t lose that strong basic connection. The two go together: service to Prabhupāda’s mission enables us to understand higher things, and when we do understand higher things, we become more enlivened to serve in his preaching mission. My dear Śrīla Prabhupāda, I am grateful to understand that my connection to you is the most important thing in my life. *Yasya prasāda bhagavata prasādo.* Whether I get higher realization or not, I must attain and keep my *guru-niṣṭhā* and my service connection to you.

That also means personal affection, seeing your *mūrti* form, reading your books, relishing your way of saying things (as in *Kṛṣṇa* book). Keeping alive in that. Appreciating your particular stamp on things. Associating with your devotees, reminding them about Prabhupāda—these are some things I am thinking of.

## 4.6: October 20

I missed writing here for two days in a row.

I have been serving Prabhupāda in the temple by teaching the classes, and tonight I had a meeting with my disciples. I was feeling humbled by the class I had heard earlier that made us aware of our fallen, hopeless condition. Our only hope is to be picked up by guru and Kṛṣṇa. To hold a disciples’ meeting after that seemed a little contradictory. How can I assume to be a guru?

I spoke on the topic of humility and tried to purify myself, as well as to remind the devotees of the importance of a humble demeanor. I read the section from *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* where Dabir Khasa and Sakara Mallika approach Lord Caitanya and ask that He please accept them. I tried to remind us all of our disqualifications in this lifetime. Our sinful activities before we joined this movement have a permanent effect on us. We shouldn’t become proud and think that we have any qualification. We should be aware that we are dependent on the mercy of Prabhupāda and Kṛṣṇa. Not that we should become depressed, but rather we should be happy that we have been given contact with this movement despite our disqualification. We should accurately assess our position as tiny, smaller than a blade of grass. That is the *mahā-vakya*, humility. Go forward in this way, vigilant against envy.

One devotee asked me whether we should avoid situations that are likely to make us proud or envious. “Yes,” I said, “but how can we avoid all those situations? Sometimes we will be honored because of the service we do, so we have to remain humble.” I guess being a spiritual master is one of those situations that are likely to make us proud or envious. There are so many risks we have to take.

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda, today I asked a devotee how to pray to you. The answer I got was that we should speak in prayers composed by the previous *ācāryas.* I thought one of the most obvious prayers is the *praṇāma-mantra* which you yourself gave to us. “I offer my obeisances to His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda, who is very dear to Kṛṣṇa on this earth, having taken shelter at the lotus feet of the transcendental Lord.” And sometimes we pray to you spontaneously in our own words, “Prabhupāda, please help me,” “Prabhupāda, thank you for allowing me to serve and function as your assistant.”

Now we are trying to pray that you give us the mercy of your association eternally. We have to realize our eternal relationship with you in the spiritual world. It is difficult, Prabhupāda, because I don’t want to be pretentious. Yet we must do this. Please guide me, Śrīla Prabhupāda, to serve you in your *sādhaka* form as the founder-*ācārya* of ISKCON, as the dear person who was so kind to me in this life, who gave me so much association and who sustains me now. But also guide me to worship you in your eternal spiritual form. I appear to be quite experienced in ISKCON, but when I think of my relationship with you in your eternal spiritual form and *rasa* with Kṛṣṇa, then I realize I am completely inexperienced. I am a baby, a neophyte. That is one of the reasons I hesitate to inquire into it, because I don’t want to give up my prestigious position as a teller of Prabhupāda stories and an old devotee. But I am willing to do that also. I am willing to go ahead and admit that I know very little, I don’t know the Sanskrit terms for all the stages of *rasa*, I don’t know your name in the spiritual world, I don’t know anything. But I aspire to be with you and to serve you. However, I have to prove myself by continual, solid service to you in your *sādhaka* form and in your ISKCON movement. As you write in so many purports, by preaching we attract the attention of Kṛṣṇa. Let me preach and please you in that way. Then you will reveal to me the higher truths. Please protect me.

I went and saw Nārāyaṇa Mahārāja again today. He gave me real encouragement for my remembrances of Prabhupāda. He said that we should always remember our *gurudeva* in separation from him. To remember him properly, we shouldn’t emphasize the awe and reverence or the fear, but we should think of him as our near and dear friend. Then, thinking of him in separation, we should weep. He said this is so important, but unfortunately we neglect it. We have so many other things to do, but we should arrange our lives in such a way to increase our remembrance in separation from Prabhupāda. And how should we think of Prabhupāda? We should think about his pastimes and his activities, how he was kind to us. He was talking about Prabhupāda in the form that we knew him, in the *sādhaka* form. This is why I am encouraged.

It made perfect sense to me. Although we want to know Prabhupāda in his eternal form, and although it is certainly true that Prabhupāda will be pleased by our advancement into the higher stages of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, just the plain remembrance of Prabhupāda as I have been doing was also being mentioned today as very important. How else can we feel separation except that we are parted from the person we knew? If we don’t know Prabhupāda in his eternal form, we can yearn for that realization, but can only yearn for Prabhupāda through our experience of him. Remembering Prabhupāda as we knew him is definitely a form of *vipralambha.* And when it increases and increases, when we think of Prabhupāda as our “near and dear friend,” the person who helped us so much in spiritual life, then we will learn to cry for Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa. Otherwise, we can’t. I took this as a very valuable and encouraging validation of my Prabhupāda recall work. Now I pray to continue with my remembrances of Prabhupāda and to always increase my love for him.

I am dictating this a few nights before the beginning of Kārttika, the special month to increase our love of Kṛṣṇa. I am happy to be here in Vṛndāvana, to be teaching the course, and to be experiencing real auspiciousness in my life. Auspiciousness means there are deep changes happening; I have to be careful and not make a mistake. Enthusiasm should be guided by *śruti-smṛti-purāṇādi-pañcaratra-vidhiṁ vinā.*

Prabhupāda, please let me serve you, please let me remember you, please guide me. I want to especially concentrate on that prayer—please guide me.

Prabhupāda, please let me go forward in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Please let me go forward as far as possible into this mood of praying to the *mañjarīs,* reading the rasika literature again and again, being attracted to Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa in Their conjugal pastimes, and developing greed.

Prabhupāda, I know that these things are all confidential. You yourself were so confidential, private, and grave that you didn’t even tell us about it. You knew that preaching to us meant stressing the *Bhagavad-gītā* and convincing us that God exists. But you also gave us the “post-graduate study,” *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,* and beyond that, *Caitanya-caritāmṛta.* You gave us the truth that Kṛṣṇa came to taste Rādhā’s love in the form of Lord Caitanya. You explained to us that Lord Caitanya wanted to distribute that love, the love of the *gopīs,* to the whole world. You introduced us to the Six Gosvāmīs. But you were also careful. You didn’t want to confuse us. You wanted to protect us.

Prabhupāda, please bless us to be ready for higher Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Let us pursue love of God and enhance our faithfulness to you. Let us become better servants in your mission by becoming touchstones, or the moons you wish you had in your star-filled sky. That is our whole purpose, dear Śrīla Prabhupāda.

## 4.6: October 22

Tonight in my *Kṛṣṇa* book class, I read a letter by Prabhupāda in which he criticized a crude sketch of Kṛṣṇa stealing the *gopīs’* clothes. This sketch originally appeared in a 1967 *Back to Godhead.*

I have been trying to give Prabhupāda’s version of everything. He said we shouldn’t go only to *kṛṣṇa-līlā,* but we should study all the pastimes of Kṛṣṇa. If we hear some teachings that are different from what Prabhupāda usually taught, we have to understand why Prabhupāda taught according to time and place, and appreciate and accept it. Under-stand deeply what he said. If, for example, he said something for beginners, that doesn’t mean we will always be beginners, and therefore, that particular instruction is the only relevant one for our lives. He gave more advanced instructions too. Similarly, the spiritual master may say not to read the Tenth Canto, but he means we should first qualify our-selves before reading it. There are many statements by Prabhupāda that describe the Tenth Canto as the perfection of our reading. Kṛṣṇa in Vṛndāvana.

It is so nice to be able to do things Prabhupāda’s way and to hear his lectures.

Prabhupāda, your devotees in different parts of the world are trying to serve you and to realize Kṛṣṇa consciousness. I heard that one Godbrother has taken a Kārttika vow to chant one hundred and eight rounds a day. I know another devotee in Bombay who works for the BBT. He is on a marathon morning till night. He doesn’t attend *maṅgala-ārati* because he is working so hard to print your books. He has to supply all the temples so that they will have enough books for the Prabhupāda marathon. Another devotee is going on an extensive tour of cities to chant Hare Kṛṣṇa with a band. Other devotees are teaching in the *gurukulas.* Everyone is trying to get your mercy, Prabhupāda, trying to please you and be happy by surrendering themselves in this way. I am not the only one meditating on Prabhupāda and trying to surrender by overcoming all doubts, I am not the only one who loves Prabhupāda.

Prabhupāda, please protect this lowly servant who wants to be true to you. I want to take help from advanced devotees who are favorable to you and to my own Kṛṣṇa consciousness. I am staying close to you; I want to be sensitive to the way you teach. You are my teacher.

## 4.6: October 24

I gave the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* class this morning. I was enthusiastic because I was prepared to speak on the verse. I knew I would be depending on Prabhupāda’s purports and statements. It is certainly enlivening to be talking to Prabhupāda’s disciples, including some of his senior men. Because I was confident and had interesting subject matter from Prabhupāda’s books, they were attentive. I saw them looking up with interest.

I gave Prabhupāda’s example from the Tenth Canto of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* where he says, “Let there be a temple.” He explains that the Lord glances at His pure devotees and injects the desire for devotional service into their hearts. I also mentioned Prabhupāda’s purports about the *tamāla* tree in the courtyard and how it is flourishing. I was able to look up while lecturing and see the *tamāla* tree before me with its rich green leaves and branches. It is filling the whole courtyard and pressing up through the screen on the ceiling. I turned to Prabhupāda’s purport in the First Canto, the section about the talks between Nārada and Vyāsa, where he says that everything, every bit of knowledge, should be used in the service of Kṛṣṇa. He mentioned psychology and art, even fiction writers and litterateurs. It is inspiring to see how everything—science, philosophy, psychology—should be used in the service of Kṛṣṇa. At a time when senior ISKCON devotees are looking for esoteric subject matter and higher studies, it was also assuring to be able to lecture on such a basic thing as how to use the creative urge in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. And to be able to take it straight from Prabhupāda with enthusiasm and confidence, and to get some interesting response from the listeners.

So it was a rare day, a good day, because in the morning I was able to lecture on Prabhupāda’s teachings.

## 4.6: October 26

Bhūrijana asked me if I could teach a course on Prabhupāda next semester. I have been teaching courses regularly, and usually they take months to prepare. I wasn’t planning on teaching next time. He suggested I teach a course on Prabhupāda with minimum preparation, just basing it on *Śrīla Prabhupāda-līlāmṛta* and the poetry synopsis, *Remembering Śrīla Prabhupāda.* I like the idea. It would be nice to read remembrances of Prabhupāda and have devotees speak their realizations without much preparation.

Prabhupāda remembrance is like going to a *kuṇḍa.* It takes a little effort to get into a bus or car, arrive at the *kuṇḍa,* put on your *gamchā* and bathe. There may be turtles in the water and *bābājīs* who stare. We should disregard the austerities involved and just get the benefit of taking the water on our heads and bathing.

This is a *kuṇḍa.* You choose a Prabhupāda remembrance. Then you go down the steps of remembrance, counting down from the top, deeper and deeper—ten, nine, eight, seven, six—trying to remember Śrīla Prabhupāda as you go into the water. When you reach the bottom step you lean forward into the water and begin to swim. Wait for the first memory to come. Relax into it. Float into it.

Here in Vṛndāvana we tend to remember Prabhupāda’s very last days, how he stayed on the bed and was cut off from us. We didn’t know much about Prabhupāda’s inner spiritual life. He was always so grave, and he didn’t speak to us about it. He just hinted now and then that he would go when Kṛṣṇa wanted him to go. Devotees said, “Prabhupāda, your palace is ready for you in New Vrin-daban. Prabhupāda responded, “We will see which palace I go to.” And he spoke occasionally that he had promised Kṛṣṇa he would be enthusiastic to the end. He prayed for that. He continued to inspire us, instructing us to preach in this world. Prabhupāda was so compassionate, and we were so depen-dent on him to go on with his mission. It was a touching situation. We had so many heavy feelings about him leaving. Some devotees were even angry at Kṛṣṇa for taking him.

But the real meaning of Prabhupāda’s disappearance is that he re-entered Kṛṣṇa’s *līlā.* We are happy to think of him again joining Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes in eternal Vṛndāvana, even though we know so little about it. How can we continue to lament over our own loss? And yet we do, and that is natural.

We see in such a limited way when we talk of Prabhupāda memories. We can spice those memories with the knowledge we get from *śāstra,* to the degree that we realize it, about the eternal nature of the spiritual master. In other words, there is the *prakat,* or Prabhupāda’s manifested pastimes, and there is the *aprakat,* or his unmanifested pastimes. For myself, I only know what I saw and witnessed. I only know what I am able to comprehend as his disciple. If I have faith that Prabhupāda is eternal guru, *nitya-līlā praviṣṭa,* then I should understand with faith that he goes back to the spiritual world. My faith should help me follow him to the spiritual world. I should also acknowledge on faith that he never left the spiritual world.

Whatever lacking or emptiness I feel in my relationship with Prabhupāda is not an isolated lack of faith. It is symptomatic of my lack of absolute faith in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. I see a lack of faith in my chanting, in my attraction to the pastimes of Kṛṣṇa, in the movement, and in the efficacy of preaching, everything. Everything has to be strengthened.

I definitely don’t like to remember Prabhupāda’s last days, how painful they were and how dry, how embarrassing it was to be worried about my own bodily condition—the heat, my suffering in so many ways. I suffered in my relationships with my Godbrothers, and I especially suffered due to my inability to reach out to Prabhupāda in more intimate ways. It was good to go through those things and be forced to face the reality of my surrender. At least it was good to endure it so that I can have the memory, the impression of being with Prabhupāda in his last pastimes.

Sitting in his room playing those very small *karatālas.* He didn’t talk much, and then suddenly he would talk a little, usually about preaching in different parts of the world. I can gradually remember those things, the slow routine of the day, the bell marking every hour. Little else seemed to be going on.

We would talk among ourselves outside his room. Would he get better? Time weighed heavy on our hands and we wanted to go back to the West, to our preaching, our niches and comfortable offices, or our temples somewhere else in the world. We couldn’t just stay in Vṛndāvana for months. We went back, but after a while, we wanted to return to Prabhupāda in India. I have those memories. I witnessed it. And it gives you a kind of faith. It should be used. It shouldn’t just be left alone. It should be worked at.

## 4.6: October 27

Devotees gather from different parts of the world in Vṛndāvana. Some are seeking to rise above mechanical devotional service and enter the higher appreciation. At the same time, we have been appreciating how Prabhupāda himself taught us higher realization. Today I was appreciating how Prabhupāda provided us the means to deal with the very basic doubts of a neophyte.

It occurred to me that when we read the literature of the Six Gosvāmīs about *rāgānugā-bhakti,* they are mainly addressing those who have dealt with their skepticisms and doubts. If the reader experiences too many doubts, then he shows himself ineligible. It means he lacks the greed and purification for *rāgānugā-bhakti.* In that case, he is encouraged to continue practicing *vaidhī-bhakti* until he becomes more qualified.

But the fact is, although we may be seeking higher stages, we do (at least I do) still get visited by skepticism. I occasionally have doubts even in the existence of the spiritual world and the Personality of Godhead. Now that we are discussing Kṛṣṇa’s intimate entourage, we can doubt whether Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa affairs are really taking place and are the complete goal of all spiritual life.

Where should one go with such shameful doubts? Therefore, I appreciated that Prabhupāda was always available for us to hear our most basic, obnoxious doubts. We often expressed them on morning walks in what appeared to be a mock debate. He often invited us to take the roles of scientists and theologians. Prabhupāda would say, “What do they say?” He was inviting us to bring out our own skepticism. In the name of giving scientific arguments, we eagerly expressed our doubts. Then Prabhupāda would directly confront them and defeat them. He defeated us with direct, simple logic and *śāstra.* Prabhupāda never shied away from dealing with us, even when we were doubtful; he never told us we had no right to be doubtful, but instead dismantled our skeptical thoughts and built our faith. It was refreshing.

## 4.6: October 30

Prabhupāda, tonight in my course I spoke on the *rāsa* dance. Please don’t think I spoke prematurely—we have been building ourselves up to this pastime, and I spoke just according to *Kṛṣṇa* book. We read a section of how Kṛṣṇa danced with the *gopīs,* and then, immediately afterward, the section where Mahārāja Parīkṣit asked why Kṛṣṇa should dance with other men’s wives. We gave all the answers that Śukadeva gave and then elaborated on them. Occasionally, we offered some new information from Viśvanātha Cakravartī. We gathered this information from the BBT’s *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* Tenth Canto, chapter 32, and other sources. But the foundation of everything and the main substance was your *Kṛṣṇa* book. So, Prabhupāda, I think you will be pleased in the way we heard the *rāsa* dance tonight and even discussed the pros and cons of hearing it.

I felt a glow after the class and was happy walking back to my room. This Vṛndāvana-*dhāma* is so nice. I have so many friends here. And the Guest house is a nice place for a Westerner to stay in difficult India. As I write a few lines before taking rest, I can hear Jagadīśa Mahārāja singing *“namam īśvaram …*” in the temple room, and I am satisfied. Please, Prabhupāda, let me be your devotee. Whatever I can add to my present Kṛṣṇa consciousness, any advances I make, let them just be to help me become a better disciple of yours. Let me always remember your examples and teachings.

In the *rāsa* dance class tonight, I remembered many of your examples, like the story about the cow who saw red and thought it was a fire, and another example you gave about people prematurely giving out information about Kṛṣṇa and the *gopīs*. You said even if they give us warnings, they are still not proper in giving this subject matter. You compared it to warning a young child about the dangers of a razor blade but then handing him the razor blade anyway. I just thought of one example after another, all from you, all apt and strong. Therefore, I can dare to say that I thought the class went well because it was based on you.

## 4.6: October 31

I have to decide on my airplane travel. I wonder when I can practice *rāgānugā-bhakti.* I hope I recover from my head cold—if you just go like this from one thing to another without specific praying to Prabhupāda, it’s not good.

One could ask, “How are you going to insert Prabhupāda consciousness into all these things?” You have to take time out just to think about Prabhupāda. Don’t do anything else during that time. There should be specific projects, such as writing about him, or a time when you pray to him, such as in the *samādhi-mandira.*

So keep alert and up-to-date in your Prabhupāda consciousness. You keep it up-to-date by regularly reading his books and hearing his talks and by mental practice. It means being aware of his conclusions and opinions on different matters. If you want to know, for example, how you should live in Vṛndāvana, then refer to how Prabhupāda says we should live in Vṛndāvana. In a similar way, you have to adjust to all your experiences.

I am trying to see Prabhupāda’s message more deeply for myself. How to take instructions for further stages in Kṛṣṇa consciousness? Prabhupāda told about all the stages, including the most advanced stages. It may be that he emphasized the early stages more, the work for the movement and the practice by obligation. But he also emphasized serving by *bhāva,* by enthusiasm. He told us things wouldn’t last if we based them only on some initial sense of obligation. He said we would later resent them. So now that I am hearing of the need to go beyond obligatory service, I have to appreciate that Prabhupāda wanted that too. This is an adjustment to my Prabhupāda consciousness. I don’t want to force Prabhupāda’s message into some shape of my own choosing, but neither should I think that my current state of Prabhupāda consciousness is the last word. Even ISKCON’s current consensus isn’t necessarily the last ultimate word on what Prabhupāda means. Prabhupāda is accessible to all of us through his books, yet there is both an outer and an inner meaning. Being faithful to Prabhupāda doesn’t mean just keeping the party line; it means making your own advancement, making your own changes, and at the same time, staying faithful to Prabhupāda. Always find a Prabhupāda instruction to live by. Don’t serve him out of sense gratification. Improve yourself to improve your service to Prabhupāda.

## 4.6: November 1

I was interviewed today by a devotee for the Swedish radio station, Radio Rāma. He asked me, “You have been a devotee for many years. What are some of the big changes that ISKCON is going through?” I said the biggest change occurred when Prabhupāda disappeared, and we had to try to follow him without his direct presence. All other changes seem minor compared to that. But Kṛṣṇa wanted Prabhupāda to go back to Godhead. Prabhupāda wanted to go back because he was submissive to Kṛṣṇa’s desire. But Prabhupāda also wanted us to learn how to take care of ourselves. Somehow, devotees are doing that, doing what they have to do. I mean, we are doing what we want to do, or we are doing what our karma directs us to do, and our true colors are being exposed. We couldn’t expect to remain Kṛṣṇa conscious only because Prabhupāda had us in his grasp. When you grow older, you have to practice Kṛṣṇa conscious-ness because you want to be Kṛṣṇa conscious. Everyone has to make their own choice in this regard.

Aside from this, even those who stayed in Prabhupāda’s movement and worked for its goals have made mistakes and shown material motivation. One wonders if it wouldn’t have been better if they had left the movement rather than stayed in it with so much personal motivation. So some are in and some are out, but most of his followers remember him.

It is hard to speak in a general way about Prabhupāda or to talk about many people’s appreciation for him. We feel on sure ground talking about our own appreciation for Prabhupāda, but we each have such a tiny version of him. It is good when we can appreciate Prabhupāda in some universal way. Then it has substance. Maintaining ISKCON with its book distribution and Deity worship and inter-national centers is a group effort. People work together because they want to please Prabhupāda. Even now, at a time when Prabhupāda’s disciples have disciples of their own, the central focus and motivation is still to please Prabhupāda. One group may think, “To please Prabhupāda, let us distribute books.” Another group may think, “To please Prabhupāda, let us maintain his centers.” I like to reflect on these group appreciations of Prabhupāda. At the same time, when within a group one person speaks his own realization, that can touch all of us. When we see how Prabhupāda touched a single person and how that person is able to articulate it, we can feel a part of that realization. What I want to do in Prabhupāda consciousness is to bring Prabhupāda’s presence more into the forefront.

In the last class tonight in the *Kṛṣṇa* book seminar, I advocated that people continue reading by the methods we used. I said that when there are details like the dust being raised from the hooves of the cows in Vṛndāvana at sunset, it brings us right into the picture and there we are in Goloka. We are sentient beings. When Śukadeva Gosvāmī describes something sensually, we can be there. One of the devotees asked, “Can this be done with other books besides *Kṛṣṇa* book?”

“Yes,” I said, “but analytical books don’t lend themselves as easily as the *Kṛṣṇa* book.” It made me think that we can participate more in our reading about Prabhupāda. We can try by memory and by reading Prabhupāda’s biography, and by hearing from persons who have had Prabhupāda’s association, to come into his presence. We can find him where he lived in the past on this earth. He is also present now in his *vāṇī,* and we continue to reach for him in that way. We also find him in our prayers and in our desire to understand his eternal form. But one accessible place to find him is in his pastimes when he was with us.

Every morning we go to see the golden form of Prabhupāda in the *samādhi-mandira*. No matter how many times I go, I can’t seem to keep my mind fixed on the verses of *Gurv-aṣṭakam.* Such a sad case. At least my feet stand there and my voice sings to him. After the *samādhi-mandira ārati,* we immediately go into the temple room. There we bow in front of Prabhupāda. When I look up at Prabhupāda’s form covered in a gray *cādār,* I can’t see his lotus feet. Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda, let us stay in your shelter. We see so little of Kṛṣṇa in our lives, but we want to see more.

## 4.6: November 2

I want to remember Prabhupāda while serving in the mainstream of his movement. I am confident that Prabhupāda is with Kṛṣṇa. I wish I could understand what his particular relationship with Kṛṣṇa is. We can be pretty confident that Prabhupāda’s relationship is of a particular nature like all the *ācāryas* in our *sampradāya.* But since I cannot fully understand it at present, I serve him in his *sādhaka* form by remembering him in this world, doing his work among us. That is the way to qualify to be with him in the future.

At the time of death, I will really have to depend on Prabhupāda as my *guru mahārāja* for whom I worked in this world. I may not know then where I am going next. I will just reach out my hand to him and say, “Prabhupāda, just take me where I have to go, and let me be connected with you again. If possible, I want to be with you, but I know I don’t deserve it. Nonetheless, I am dependent on your mercy. Please engage me in devotional service.”

## 4.6: November 3

Scan quickly over the day that has passed and see the most prominent Prabhupāda remembrance. It often consists of something I have heard from another devotee. This morning in the class, Tamal Krishna Mahārāja was speaking on the verse *titikṣavasuhṛda sarva-dehinām.* He gave Prabhupāda as the example of *titikṣava* Prabhupāda was so merciful that he left Vṛndāvana at an old age and went to America. I have heard this many times, but today it aroused my loyalty and appreciation for Prabhupāda as merciful.

We have to overcome our constant use of Prabhupāda’s sayings and language which may begin to sound phony. In the family of Prabhupāda, everything is for Prabhupāda’s glorification. To the degree that we are not surrendered, these things can become hackneyed. We have to be honest.

Today: there was that mention of Śrīla Prabhupāda in the class, and then I saw him in his *mūrti* form several times. In the evening we had the last meeting of the VIHE. All the teachers and students gathered together and spoke about the importance of Prabhupāda’s books. Jagadīśa Mahārāja asserted that everything we need is in Prabhupāda’s books. I thought about that and how I have to read and appreciate his books in that way. These reminders, when they are spoken by sincere devotees who themselves practice what they preach, are effective. They can help us.

Jagadīśa Mahārāja told a story about a devotee in the West who was kidnapped by a deprogrammer. Jagadīśa Mahārāja went to Prabhupāda and asked how we could stop people from attacking our members like this. Prabhupāda replied that in a war, there will be casualties on both sides. Then Prabhupāda added, “But we should train our people in such a way that they don’t become casualties.” Jagadīśa Mahārāja related this statement by Prabhupāda about reading his books, and the importance of knowing the philosophy so we don’t fall a victim to *māyā.*

In the same meeting, Prahlādānanda Mahārāja said that whenever he was with Prabhupāda, he always noticed that Prabhupāda was meditating within on Kṛṣṇa, while externally arranging to preach and to serve Kṛṣṇa.

Bhūrijana told a story about Yaśomatī-sūta, the first Chinese devotee who was recruited in Hong Kong. He said that they brought Yaśomatī-sūta to meet Prabhupāda in Māyāpura. Prabhupāda had previously told them that there is one devotee there, and somehow you can find him. Out of billions of people, they weren’t finding any, but Prabhupāda said they would find at least one. Yaśomatī-sūta was the one. When Prabhupāda met him, he ordered him to translate the *Bhagavad-gītā* into Chinese. When he finally finished translating the *Bhagavad-gītā,* he went to see Prabhupāda in Māyāpura. Prabhupāda asked him to read aloud in Chinese. As he read, Prabhupāda faced the sculptured form of Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa on the wall. He listened for some time as Yaśomatī-sūta read in Chinese and then said, “How sweet it is.”

## 4.6: November 4

I was driving in a van today with a number of devotees. It was noisy and I had a headache. I could-n’t really hear the conversation the other devotees were having, but it seemed to be a debate about whether the eternal *rasa* is something that is within us in seed form, or whether it is planted in us from an outside source.

I overheard one devotee say, “This is inconceivable, but I just have to follow whatever Prabhupāda says.” His statement penetrated the noise of the traffic and the van’s engine—this one devotee’s conviction which sounded so inspired. Of course, it is common for devotees to say they just follow what Prabhupāda says. I hear it as often as anyone, and it doesn’t usually move me. But this moved me because it was a statement of ultimate, convincing proof, especially for an inconceivable subject. There are so many references and ways to juggle the Sanskrit, but I felt reassured and inspired and thought, “This is what I want to do also.” You can’t go wrong if you base your life on what Prabhupāda says.

I am writing this about a week before Prabhupāda’s disappearance day. Devotees will be expected to speak homages about him. I feel a little intimidated by the upcoming festival when people expect one to speak eloquent praises of realization and appreciation. At first I thought, “What can I say? I feel as if I am in a transition state, and also I am not a good devotee.” But then I remembered that I could still speak simple, straight Prabhupāda-*kathā,* and that would be satisfying to everyone.

## 4.6: November 5

It’s going to be difficult to sleep tonight because of the fireworks. Amazing how in India they can get these fireworks that practically sound like bombs going off. Prabhupāda, when I get upset or jealous due to whatever reasons, I must remember to think of you, my *gurudeva,* my spiritual master.

## 4.6: November 6

Another night for loud fireworks. I don’t want to use that as an excuse why I cannot deeply meditate on Prabhupāda. The number of devotees on campus has swelled as the festival times approach. I don’t go out of my room now so much. I can’t go to Prabhupāda’s rooms or the *samādhi-mandira* anymore without it becoming a social outing. I have been staying in my room, busying myself by preparing my lectures, choosing names for initiates, and other Kṛṣṇa conscious activities.

But I am also aware that my meditation isn’t very deep right now. One thing I have to do is prepare for an introductory talk to be given in a week and a half at a session called “Churning the Nectar.” This session will be held the night before Prabhupāda’s disappearance day. Devotees will relate their memories of Prabhupāda, and I will also be expected to tell some stories.

One thing I thought of saying is how “Prabhupāda said” is the ultimate scriptural reference for Prabhupāda’s followers. Therefore, Prabhupāda meditations are important because everything comes from him. He is the basis of our faith in *Bhagavad-gītā* and spiritual life. To know about this person and the different things he did is important. We fill our days with so many busy things on Prabhupāda’s behalf, but we also have to think of him. A more advanced stage is to *always* think of him; not just that we set aside an hour a day as “Prabhupāda’s hour,” but that our whole life is spent in meditation on him. And when we do think of him, we should cry. The advanced stage is to think of him and to cry out of separation.

Braja-bihari dāsa, the manager of the VIHE, just came by and gave me some gifts as an honorarium given to teachers. One of the gifts was a tape recording of Prabhupāda singing in a recording studio in Bombay. He said it was a very rare tape. I could hear a studio organ and commercial-sounding musical instruments. Prabhupāda sang some of the *bhajanas* and, at other times, other devotees sang in chorus.

The rhythm instruments began. They were the usual, almost rowdy, sloppy, clashing drum and *karatālas* that devotees played in the early 1970s. It sounded like they were out on the street banging away. The studio atmosphere seemed to confine Prabhupāda. It sounded like he wasn’t able to sing the way he wanted to. The chorus wasn’t well balanced. It went up and down the octaves and keys. Every time the chorus sang even a little off key, it grated on my ears. That is the nature of a studio recording. It wasn’t professional at all, not like some of the studio recordings Prabhupāda did in the West.

When I caught myself analyzing it so critically, I managed to turn my mind around to appreciate the historical moment captured by this recording. It was a special recording, even though it was so unprofessional. Just to think of Prabhupāda in that studio, the great world preacher with his flock of foreign disciples, in India making a recording. Why was he there? Without knowing the details, I can easily surmise that Prabhupāda was trying to make a record for spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Despite the recording mistakes, he was singing strongly. It was a nice gift.

I was grateful that all of sudden this tape came into my room, because usually I don’t think I have time to listen to Prabhupāda singing on tape. But on the pretext of it being a new tape, I listened. It helped to drown out the fireworks. I listened carefully, sitting on the floor. I continued to play the tape while I wrote letters. Although I wasn’t close, although my listening wasn’t deep, still Prabhupāda was there, and I was there with him as he sang *bhajanas.*

## 4.6: November 7, Govardhana-pūjā

It was a busy morning. We held an initiation ceremony in the early morning in Prabhupāda’s *samādhi-mandira.* It was followed by a lecture for Govardhana-*pūjā* and then the festivities. The initiation was witnessed by the bigger-than-life-sized *mūrti* of Prabhupāda. In front of him, mounted on a golden throne, was a Govardhana-*śilā.* There was a difference of opinion among devotees whether the *śilā* should be presiding. I thought that, even without the *śilā,* we could have performed the initiation in front of Prabhupāda. That would have been sufficient. He was the witness to the initiation.

Three gurus, all disciples of Prabhupāda, held the initiation. It was like a joint family of gurus with their disciples, all at the feet of Prabhupāda. Other senior disciples of Prabhupāda were also present.

After that initiation, we moved over to the temple room, and I gave a Govardhana-*pūjā* lecture based on the *Kṛṣṇa* book. Again, all references were from his books. Hundreds of devotees were present as I read from *Kṛṣṇa* book how the Govardhana festival should be held. Five thousand years ago, Kṛṣṇa told Nanda Mahārāja how to worship Govardhana Hill. Prabhupāda instructs us in the *Kṛṣṇa* book that it should still be held in the same way, and that this ceremony is celebrated according to Kṛṣṇa’s directions in all the temples in Vṛn-dāvana. We read the instructions, and then the devotees arranged to celebrate the festival in that way. We worshiped a cow at the ISKCON Goshalla, then returned to the *mandira* and to a big *prasādam* hill and circumambulated it. Then the food was thrown. Everything was done as Prabhupāda desired.

I began to feel physically weak during the ceremonies, so I went to my room and read *Caitanya-caritāmṛta.* I read one verse and purport after another, not trying to study by any particular note-taking system or memorization. I absorbed at least a little of it. You can call this *vaidhī-bhakti* if you like, because it was done without any love. But I do have a *desire* to love and not just follow regulative practices. I do have a desire to be close to Prabhupāda. By reading his books, you get as close as anyone can get. So with those motives, I read and tried to understand *bhakti* in the verses.

## 4.6: November 8

Today, three of us went to Rādhā-kuṇḍa. Many groups of ISKCON members also went to Rādhā-kuṇḍa today, and we saw quite a few devotees there. Some devotees bathe at Rādhā-kuṇḍa, but some devotees don’t. The question has been raised whether Prabhupāda permits us to bathe in Rādhā-kuṇḍa. We know that without his being pleased by our activities, no spiritual activity can be effective for us.

Our little group bathed in Rādhā-kuṇḍa. We make our case in favor of bathing by Prabhupāda’s purports in *Nectar of Instruction.* He repeatedly recommends that one bathe in Rādhā-kuṇḍa. When Prabhupāda was present, he heard that some devotees were behaving frivolously in Rādhā-kuṇḍa. He became angry and forbade them to bathe there. We take that instruction as temporary. Prabhupāda was responding to some devotees’ misbehavior. If devotees bathe reverently and prayerfully in Rādhā-kuṇḍa in the mood Prabhupāda recommends in his books, we think it is not only permissible, but necessary to do so. One who bathes prayerfully in Rādhā-kuṇḍa can attain *kṛṣṇa-premā.* We can at least pray for *kṛṣṇa-premā.*

My purpose is not to bring up arguments as much as to demonstrate how Prabhupāda was with us as we tried to understand what he wanted us to do.

While we were there, we also read from *Nectar of Instruction* the glories of Rādhā-kuṇḍa. The very fact that Western devotees come to Rādhā-kuṇḍa is only Prabhupāda’s mercy. Sarvabhāvana Prabhu, in his dedication to *Śrī Śikṣāṣṭakam,* mentions Prabhupāda’s influence in this regard: “The Occident is today ready to receive this divine knowledge and the *Vedas* only because of the tireless and patient missionary efforts of His Divine Grace.”

## 4.6: November 10, Prabhupāda’s Disappearance Day

It is 6:40 p.m. In about twenty minutes, I have to go downstairs to Prabhupāda’s room. They have asked me to perform the *ārati.* This is an annual event here. Vṛndāvana is the best place in the world to observe Prabhupāda’s disappearance because it is the place where it happened.

I have been understanding more that Prabhupāda’s disappearance wasn’t a sad time, although it seems that way for us. Prabhupāda was going back to the spiritual world to join Kṛṣṇa’s eternal pas-times. It was blissful for him, finishing up the tremendous preaching he was doing in this world. We should have been happy for him. On the other hand, our lamentation is natural. It is also spiritual. But at the time, “spiritual happiness in separation” seemed abstract to us. We only knew our grief and our numbness in the suffering of what we thought was death.

When we remember these times, they are always mixed, and we cannot forget the pinch of it even though we learn the higher truths about the immortality of the soul and the blissful entrance of the pure devotee into his *nitya-pravistha-līlā.* We are realizing more, especially this year, how little we have known of Vaiṣṇava spiritual life and how special it is, even compared to other world religions. Only in Kṛṣṇa consciousness do we know about the conjugal pastimes of Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa. Only now are we finding out that Prabhupāda came to teach those pastimes to us. He can lead us back to that specific goal (back to Godhead). We have started to become more educated even as we feel the simple emotions of a disciple of Prabhupāda.

In any case, I am very much a neophyte in my devotion to Prabhupāda. It is he who is blessing me, not that I am rewarding him in any way by being loyal. Remember: Don’t think that you are so wonderful and that you are a great credit to your spiritual master. The fact is that he picked you up and he continues to maintain you despite your fallen background. Prabhupāda’s mercy is all I am made of. He has Kṛṣṇa, and I am running after him calling, “Kṛṣṇa! Kṛṣṇa!”

I’m going to go downstairs and spend the time watching the moments go by as we approach the exact hour the *ārati* should be performed in your honor. The *ārati* will be performed to your bed which will be completely covered with flowers. Devotees will be packed into every possible space in your room. It won’t resemble the grief-stricken scene of 1977. We will all be solemn, but, at the same time, satisfied to be serving you in separation. I pray to be detached from my bodily needs and absorbed in the needs of the soul.

Prabhupāda, you were a liberated soul. Please help me to understand that I am also eternally liberated, although I am now covered by this body and its attachments. Therefore, I cannot see what is the goal of life. Please save me, please liberate me in your devotional service.

Now, it is after the *ārati*. Where has he gone? He has left us. Yet he has gone back to Godhead. During the *ārati* I felt some of these mixed emotions. I think all the devotees did. The bed was beautifully decorated with flowers, but there were no flowers in 1977. The decorative flower bed commemorates his entrance into Goloka Vṛn-dāvana.

We were infants crying in 1977. Prabhupāda meant everything to us; we depended on him so much for our spiritual life. Now he was going away. We had to learn, painfully, how to depend on his instructions and not just on his personal presence.

Tonight it felt good to be in the company of stalwarts and to accept everyone as Prabhupāda’s followers—Hari-śauri, Mahānidhi Swami, Tamal Krishna Mahārāja. Bhūrijana was going around with Prabhupāda’s *cādār* placing it on everyone’s head. Jagadīśa Mahārāja was singing. To be in their company was special. We all felt bereft. We all lost our father.

I also felt how little we experience of reality, whether material or spiritual. We can only hear such a small range of sounds and we have such limited vision. In spiritual life, we can only experience a drop of the ocean. So, during his actual disappearance, all we knew was the grief of children being separated from their father. Although we tend to think that now, in 1991, we know more, I wonder if we really do. Why aren’t tears constantly flowing from our eyes? Where is our emotional depth?

Again I thought of my own mortality and the mortality of those in the room. The flowers on Prabhupāda’s bed will be gone by tomorrow, or at least their freshness will have faded. Our freshness has already faded, and soon, one by one, we will all be gone. And then we will all come back again. For whoever is born, death is certain; for whoever dies, birth is certain. One of these times we may join Prabhupāda in the world he was pointing us toward, the world Kṛṣṇa was showing to Balarāma: “See, my dear Lord Balarāma, this wonderful Vṛndāvana forest … “

## 4.6: November 11

We went to a function at the Gauḍīya Math today. The speaker introduced the history of Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavism as it descended from Lord Caitanya down through Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura and Bhakti-siddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura. He said, “ISKCON is also part of Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavism, but unfortunately, you say that you are not. You don’t admit that you are part of Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavism, and I am sorry that you say this.” As soon as the speaker said that, several of us denied his statements. ISKCON is proud to claim affiliation with Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavism. It turned out that this speaker had heard from an inexperienced devotee back in 1967 that ISKCON doesn’t admit connection with the Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavism.

The talks were mostly in Hindi. I thought we might be invited to speak, but the audience was mainly Hindi-speaking. Still, anticipating that I might lecture, I began to prepare some points.

I planned to explain that ISKCON certainly admits our connection with Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavism. Prabhupāda taught us that from the beginning. Anyone in ISKCON who says we are not Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavas is misinformed. Perhaps, however, some of the members of the Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇava movement don’t recognize ISKCON as connected to them. Prabhupāda warned us about this. This may have caused feelings of alienation in ISKCON from the family of Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavism, but it is just a superficial alienation. There were people during Prabhupāda’s time who couldn’t accept the Western-born *brāhmaṇas* and *sannyāsīs,* and they didn’t accept other differences in Prabhupāda’s preaching, such as his allowing women to join and become initiated disciples. Some members of the Gauḍīya societies have also been disturbed because ISKCON addresses our spiritual master as Prabhupāda.

Anyway, both sides should recognize that ISKCON is part of Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavism and that we in ISKCON do hope to find friendly reception among the Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavas. We know also that we are not very qualified and that we ISKCON members should not be proud of our Western background at all. We should be very humble. Prabhupāda always taught us that we are not this body. So no one—whether Indian or American—should think, “I am American Vaiṣṇava,” “I am Indian Vaiṣṇava,” “I am Indian Gosvāmī,” “I am American Gosvāmī.” I rehearsed my speech, but never got a chance to give it.

Still, I was glad to be thinking of Prabhupāda’s strong teachings. The speakers’ lectures were in the context of the Gauḍīya Math, and they were filled with references to the preaching that has gone on there. My references were to Prabhupāda. While I sat there thinking over the Hindi, I was happy in two ways: happy that I was at the Gauḍīya Math on Prabhupāda’s behalf, something I think would have pleased him very much. He would be happy to see me trying to experience the real India rather than always looking only for Western amenities and comforts. He would be pleased to see me becoming familiar with the ritual practices of the Gauḍīya temples. ISKCON has its own ways of doing things, even eating and *acamana* and particular *bhajanas* that we sing with their particular tunes. I think Prabhupāda would be satisfied to see us learning this other way. So I felt like a Prabhupāda man in that sense.

On the other hand, I maintained my ISKCON Prabhupāda identity, and that also made me feel good. These functions are always tedious since they are almost completely in Hindi. And it *was* difficult for me to be away from the amenities I am accustomed to in the ISKCON temple, which is geared more to us Westerners. But going to the Gauḍīya Math was definitely an occasion to be Prabhupāda conscious and to be aware that I am really only made of Prabhupāda’s teachings and direction. He picked us up, and he allows us to function as devotees. Other Vaiṣṇavas may see us as oddities, they may even reject us, but we remain a special hybrid created by Prabhupāda. Nothing can change that identity. I can learn so many things about the advanced nature of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, but I will always remain Prabhupāda’s creation. Those who realize that and want to protect our Prabhupāda consciousness are our real friends.

## 4.6: November 16

I have been reciting prayers in the early morning, starting with prayers to Prabhupāda. I have been feeling attracted to praying without recording my words in writing because this eliminates the idea of praying in public. Praying with the subtle idea in the back of my mind that one day an audience may read these prayers removes some of the genuineness of the experience.

I have gone over this with myself many times. Since I am a writer, I tend to see everything in my life as writing material, even if it’s prayer or *japa* or temple worship. Anyway, I have come to terms with it, and I see my dovetailing of the creative urge with my cultivation of inner life as a form of preaching.

It can be discouraging when devotees in ISKCON quarrel so much among themselves. It can also be discouraging to see the strong conservative element in ISKCON that doesn’t look kindly upon anything that doesn’t follow the straight and narrow path they have chalked out. I want to look at these things with charity. After all, everyone *is* trying to please Prabhupāda. I can only hope that we all become free of our *anarthas* and learn to work together to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness, as Prabhupāda desired.

I am actually coming to enjoy going to the *samādhi-mandira* at 4 a.m. I relish seeing that large golden form of Prabhupāda. It is cold enough now that Prabhupāda not only wears a *cādār,* but the *cādār* is put over his head. This morning he looked so dark and mysterious with the dim light shining on the golden form and the grey *cādār* draped around him.

I can never remember to concentrate on the *“Gurv-aṣṭakam*” prayers and see their relationship with Prabhupāda, but at least I am there, looking up to Prabhupāda. The Prabhupāda mood is so strong at that time in the morning that even a dull person can experience it. After his *ārati,* we hurry to the temple, again bow to his *cādār* wrapped form, and by his grace, approach Rādhā-Śyāmasundara. This is the way to begin the day.

## 4.6: November 18

It is now Bhiṣma-*pañcaka,* the last five days of Kārttika. I went alone to offer a lamp to Rādhā-Śyāmasundara, and then returned alone. As I walked past Prabhupāda’s house, I had a sudden desire to go inside. *“Go in the room, there is no one there.*”

The room was fully lit with all the chandeliers on. The caretaker, Nṛsiṁha-devī, was in the entranceway with another lady where they sell books and paraphernalia. Prabhupāda’s rooms themselves were empty. I went behind the velvet cord which keeps people from getting too close. I went right up beside Prabhupāda and sat opposite him at his little desk. I almost leaned my elbows on the desk to look at him and just be with him, but then thought that was too familiar. I felt the limits of the *mūrti.* The *mūrti* represents just a frozen moment of Prabhupāda. The *mūrti* is genuinely Prabhupāda, but it is not the flowing, living, talking Prabhupāda with his tremendous presence that could evoke so much awe and fear and love and trust in us.

We have become accustomed to the *mūrti,* and like everyone, I am grateful to have this form of Prabhupāda now. I looked at this *mūrti* in Prabhupāda’s Vṛndāvana rooms and thought, “You are the boss. I am trying to become a better devotee of Your Divine Grace. I want to deepen my understanding of your books.” Then I tried to remember the old days. I looked at the letter on Prabhupāda’s desk. It was a letter Prabhupāda wrote while at the Krishna-Balaram Mandir to Rameśvara. “Hmmm, those were funny days.” I couldn’t help but let a little feeling of irony pass through me. Still, those days were genuine, and they have become part of Prabhupāda’s history. Probably every great guru has had some kind of difficulties in trying to conduct a movement; probably all great gurus have worked with disciples who helped them, but who later couldn’t follow anymore.

My mood was not so deep, but I was happy to have a few moments alone with Prabhupāda. I kept sitting there trying to meditate on him, but then I decided I could benefit by visiting an ISKCON museum, so I began to look at all the different displays in the rooms. In one case, they have things like the last piece of soap Prabhupāda used, and in various cases around the room, things like an envelope containing the last *brāhmaṇa* thread he wore, the slippers he wore on the airplane on his last trip to London, some homemade toothpaste, and so on. I recollected how one of my Godbrothers who was Prabhupāda’s secretary at that time said that he gave away most of Prabhupāda’s remnants, but he kept Prabhupāda’s tooth for himself. He now wears a reticule around his neck containing that tooth. I don’t have any wonderful remnants of Prabhupāda that I can keep near me. There must be something I can have. The thought even passed through my mind that maybe I could break into one of the museum cabinets and take something, but they are all locked. But I wanted something tangible to keep with me.

In one of the museum cases, I saw the three original volumes of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam.* They were signed by Prabhupāda with his notes inside. One case had no padlock. I tried to pull it open, but it was locked. I do have my own *Bhāgavatam* at home, yet I was grasping for something more tangible to confirm my allegiance to Prabhupāda, my discipleship. I wandered into another room, looked at the palanquin that carried Prabhupāda, at the rocking chair he used to sit in under the *tamāla* tree in his last days, a tape recorder that he used in 1977, some swabs used for cleaning the ears, pills, some conch shells which were said to be used for massaging him. I also went to his bed, placed my head at the foot of it, at the spot where I sat in the last hours.

I had been there no more than five or six minutes when another devotee came in. He was a young devotee, and he began chanting loud *japa.* That was the end of my reverie. I stayed a little longer in one of the other rooms where there is another Prabhupāda *mūrti*, but then decided to go upstairs to be alone. Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda, please let us remember you and serve you.

## 4.6: Vṛndāvana, November 20, 1991

I went to Brahmanda *Ghat* where the Vraja-*maṇḍala parikrama* devotees are a hundred strong, gathered on their next to last day of *parikrama*. I spoke there from Prabhupāda’s books. After the lecture, the devotees discussed how rare it is to enter Vṛndāvana. One devotee related how Brahmā and Uddhava and others prayed that in many, many lifetimes in the future, they might take birth here so that they could associate with the dust of Vṛndāvana. Then I asked, “If it’s so rare, how is it that we are able to enter?” The devotee replied that the dust of Vṛndāvana shouldn’t be taken in an external sense; it means attaining service to the Vaiṣṇavas, the associates of Kṛṣṇa. In other words, entrance is not just physical entrance.

Then Lokanātha Mahārāja answered the question in another way: we have access to Vṛndāvana, even though so many great souls don’t, by the mercy of Prabhupāda. When he said that, several devotees said, *“Jai!*” It’s as if none of us had thought of that. We were speaking as if we don’t have entrance, but Lokanātha Mahārāja reminded us that we do have access by Prabhupāda’s grace. It was a nice moment of Prabhupāda remembrance. Who else could have dragged us to Vṛndāvana from our Western backgrounds and mentalities? And even if we had come here on our own somehow, we would have seen only poor, rural, backward India. But now we see flashes of *kṛṣṇa-līlā* and *kṛṣṇa-premā,* and we appreciate the dust of Vṛndāvana, by Prabhupāda’s grace.

## 4.6: November 21, Last day of Kārttika

Bhūrijana and I went out today. On our way back, he walked me to the bottom of the guest house stairs. Suddenly the lights went out. While we were waiting for them to come back on, I told him an anecdote about Prabhupāda. When I was with Prabhupāda in India in 1974, the lights would often go out. One morning I said to Prabhupāda that, in India, things don’t work as well materially, and yet they have spiritual advancement. But in America things work well, but there’s no spiritual knowledge. Prabhupāda liked my statement. Then when we went on the morning walk, he started by saying, “What did you say to me about India?” I repeated it, and Prabhupāda discussed that point on his walk. As I told this anecdote, the lights went on again.

Bhūrijana told me that yesterday he spent several hours speaking with Abhirāma Prabhu who was telling many intimate memories of Prabhupāda in his last days in 1977. He said just listening to that reminded him of Prabhupāda’s great spirit of giving everything for the mission of Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

There are many important esoteric books to read in Kṛṣṇa consciousness that weren’t published during Prabhupāda’s time. Prabhupāda has also encouraged us to read some of them, such as *Kṛṣṇa Bhāvanāmṛta*, which he recommends in the *Kṛṣṇa* book, and other books he recommends in purports to *Caitanya-caritāmṛta*. But still, Prabhupāda’s own books are strong contenders for our best attention. They can give us all the nectar of *mādhurya-rasa.* It was so nice on the Vraja *parikrama* this morning, to stop several times and read from the *Kṛṣṇa* book about the *gopīs’* comments on Kṛṣṇa’s flute, and what Kṛṣṇa said to Balarāma about the glories of Vṛndāvana.

Prabhupāda has given us such an enjoyable life, but the real center should be our discipleship. This is the standard of pure devotional service. The *gopīs* in Goloka Vṛndāvana have this focus to perfection. They try to attract Kṛṣṇa because ultimately they want to please Him; they know He will be pleased by them. Similarly, let me be happy in Kṛṣṇa consciousness for Prabhupāda’s pleasure. Let me share that happiness as a preacher-disciple reporting to my spiritual master how we are all pushing on his movement together.

## 4.6: November 22

Tonight I gave a class to the older *gurukula* boys. In their regular evening class they are reading *The Hare Kṛṣṇa Explosion* by Hayagrīva dāsa. Although they usually just read it without comments, I stopped here and there to make comments to keep up their interest.

I read the section about the first initiation in New York City. I first asked the boys what the name of the storefront was before it became a temple. One boy said, “Matchless Gifts.” I said that almost all Prabhupāda’s pastimes from these early days emphasize his compassion and patience. He took great risks to bring Kṛṣṇa consciousness to us. He was more interested in preaching than in following the rules and regulations. And that’s the real meaning of all the funny stories about Prabhupāda—that we meditate and appreciate what he had to do and how great he was to bring us Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

When I got to the part about the beads, I lifted my beads out of the bead bag and showed them the big, red beads that Prabhupāda gave us. New York City is not like Vṛndāvana where you can go to the market and buy *tulasī* beads and *tilaka* and Deities. Prabhupāda had to improvise everything. It was fun to read that section and to tell how Prabhupāda was depending on Kṛṣṇa in the early days.

“Any questions?” They had the typical kind of questions little boys have: “Do you have the same string in your beads from 1966?” Another boy asked, “How did they make the first beads that we got?” He couldn’t quite understand when I said that in New York City, there was a store called Tandy’s, and that we bought the beads there. He was an Indian boy.

After the class, young Jaya Gurupāda walked with me to my room. He’s a very sincere, softhearted boy. He told me he thought it was really nice when I said that Prabhupāda depended on Kṛṣṇa in the early days.

## 4.6: November 23

I had some time today, so I read in several of Prabhupāda’s books selected sections about Kṛṣṇa in Vṛndāvana and *mādhurya-līlā*. I read about Lord Caitanya expressing the mood of Rādhārāṇī at the Ratha-yātrā in *Caitanya-caritāmṛta,* and I looked randomly through other sections too. But I also put in some good time with Prabhupāda’s purports. I found it encouraging that Prabhupāda wrote about things which are in the mood I have been pursuing lately. He said that Rādhārāṇī is so important that it appears that Kṛṣṇa is Her decoration. Rādhārāṇī is the enchanter of the enchanter of Cupid, and the residents of Vṛndāvana still call out, “*Jaya* Rādhe.”

Prabhupāda also describes how Lord Caitanya engaged in a kind of competition with Lord Jagan-nātha at the Ratha-yātrā. Lord Caitanya would go behind the cart, signifying that Rādhārāṇī wanted to see whether Kṛṣṇa forgot Her. When Lord Cait-anya did this, Lord Jagannātha would stop in His cart, and when Lord Caitanya would go ahead (signifying that Rādhārāṇī wanted to take Kṛṣṇa back to Vṛndāvana) Jagannātha would slowly begin to move again. In his purport, Prabhupāda says that in this competition, Lord Caitanya came out victorious. This means that Lord Caitanya was dis-playing the mood of Rādhārāṇī. This is what He came to do, He came to teach us service to Rādhārāṇī. That is a hidden message. Anyway, we see this message in Prabhupāda’s purport.

I also took time today to meet with devotees. I tried to help one devotee see through the growing pains and changes and doubts in ISKCON and its leadership. It was refreshing to serve Prabhupāda in this way, and to realize that by serving Prabhupāda by serving his devotees, the right to higher advancement becomes yours.

## 4.6: November 24

Here in Vṛndāvana I would say there are four principle *mūrtis* of Prabhupāda, although there are more deities. At least we see these four the most. The first one you see is the *mūrti* in the *samādhi-mandira*. It is bigger than life, and now in the cold he sits in the black inner sanctum, the black marble room with one light shining on him, his *cādār* placed over his head. The devotees huddle around outside on the cold marble floor singing *“Gurv-aṣṭakam.*”

Then, in the temple, there is a very handsome *mūrti* of Prabhupāda sitting high up on his *vyāsā-sana.* The third *mūrti* is on Gaura-Nitāi’s altar, the white marble Prabhupāda. He usually wears a sort of light-colored knit cap and *cādār*. He strikes me as a spritely little Prabhupāda compared to the others. He is smaller and made in a different style. Each *mūrti* has a different style, a different mood. And then the fourth Prabhupāda *mūrti* is in his rooms, sitting behind the desk using the dictaphone.

This afternoon I met with my disciples and read from Prabhupāda’s purports to *Ādi-līlā,* Chapter nine, about the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement. In his purports, Prabhupāda describes Lord Caitanya’s tree of Kṛṣṇa consciousness spreading all over the universe. Then he describes ISKCON as a branch on that tree. So I gave my point of view about preaching in ISKCON and how everything is part of the preaching movement.

Another devotee of Prabhupāda might say it in a different way—he might emphasize book distribution more than other forms of preaching; he might strongly criticize ISKCON members who don’t preach. My approach is more to encourage all ISKCON members to feel that they *are* preaching. I also like to encourage the leaders to encourage others.

During that class I answered questions and referred to a letter Prabhupāda wrote. This is a letter in which he said we shouldn’t say one service is better than another. That is childish. But it depends on our sincerity. We should try to be enthusiastic and perfect our own service. It was meaningful to grasp one of Prabhupāda’s instructions and convey it to others. Everyone benefits from this kind of exchange. This is our whole preaching life: to actually remember something or read it, and with conviction based on our own practices, give it to others for their benefit.

That’s a wonderful chapter *(C.c. Ādi,* Chapter 9). Prabhupāda was himself such a great preacher. His words are inspiring. He says that everyone should try to use this life to help others by saving people from birth, death, and suffering. Give them Kṛṣṇa consciousness, especially the chanting of the holy name and the nectarean love of Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa which Lord Caitanya exemplified and taught. The followers of Lord Caitanya eat this nectarean fruit, become intoxicated by it, then give it to others. This is how everyone will become merged in love of God.

## 4.6: November 28, Bombay

The *vyāsāsana* in the Bombay temple looks like a marble gazebo. Prabhupāda wore nice, shiny silk in the warm weather. Many flowers were placed in front of him. I noticed one of the ladies touching Prabhupāda’s feet—both feet, first the right and then the left. I was offering the *guru-pūjā ārati* at the time*.*

Usually I don’t like seeing people touching Prabhupāda’s feet. We never touched his feet like this when he was present, and I tend to think it is excessive when people do it with the *mūrti.* It’s too much physical contact. These thoughts went through my mind this morning as I offered the *ārati,* but then I realized my opinion has changed. I want more affectionate contact with Prabhupāda. So starting today in Bombay, I decided to touch his feet too. When I finished the *ārati,* I touched Prabhupāda’s feet, and it felt right. After all, the *mūrti* is like that—it offers more accessibility than we might have with the great soul in person. Touching the feet of the guru is a long-held tradition in Vaiṣṇava culture. We didn’t touch Prabhupāda’s feet when he was with us mostly because we didn’t dare. We had so much respect and even a little fear of him. But now, after his disappearance, we can increase our affectionate exchanges with him through the *mūrti.*

Two *Bhāgavatam* classes are given at the same time in the Bombay temple, one in English and one in Hindi. The Hindi class is given in the temple room and the English class is given in Prabhupāda’s quarters on the fifth floor of the hotel tower. The acoustics are good in there, so there is no need to use a microphone. The devotees all sit around Prabhupāda, and he oversees everything. It made me feel happy to speak near him like that. The verse was about how the material nature is very entangling and complicated. I asserted that we need to be grateful to Prabhupāda for taking us out of the entanglement. He has given us freedom all the way up to *prayojana* or pure love of Kṛṣṇa.

Bombay is a very busy temple. The phone rings constantly. I feel distracted from my attempt to study. Neither have I had much time to think of memories of Prabhupāda. I did go to Prabhupāda’s quarters this morning to give the class, and saw his *tulasī* beads and books. A glimpse of Prabhupāda’s presence flashed by me on my way in. But perhaps I can go back later and linger over some of those museum pieces.

From the fourth floor room where I am staying, I can see into the busy activity of this ISKCON Juhu Beach Hotel temple complex. I saw a truck roll in with BBT on the side, and then another *saṅkīrtana* bus come in. The hotel itself is quite busy with a whole fleet of auto rickshaws parked outside. I got a sense of the business of it, the *development* of it—of the money that’s available now in this Bombay institution. Although I was not part of the early days, I remember being with Prabhupāda here in 1974. The devotees here were struggling so much in those early days; things have developed so much.

## 4.6: November 30

Virabahu Prabhu gave the English *Bhāgavatam* class today. He commented that we would never have accepted Kṛṣṇa consciousness as anything more than “Indian mythology” except for Śrīla Prabhupāda. We might have found some translations of Jīva Gosvāmī’s books or a translation of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* by chance, because many of us were reading so many books. But we would never have taken these books or this philosophy as a nonsectarian, universal teaching. And who would have ever dreamed of dressing like a Hare Kṛṣṇa devotee or shaving his head like this? Virabahu said, “Maybe after we died, we would have considered having our corpse dressed like this, but we would never have appeared in public like this alive.” It was Śrīla Prabhupāda alone who had the power to tear us away from our worldly entanglement and engage us in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Vira-bahu Prabhu also made the point that Prabhupāda had two tasks before him when he came to the West: to encourage detachment from matter, and to instill attachment to Kṛṣṇa. It is very rare that someone could have accomplished both of those tasks, but Prabhupāda was successful.

This is the beginning of the December book distribution marathon. In his class, Virabahu Prabhu was leading up to praising Prabhupāda’s books and asserting their importance in transforming people. Therefore, we should distribute them to others.

Let me think how else I was with Prabhupāda today. I bowed to his picture when I offered *prasādam*—the small picture of him at the back of the Indian edition of *Nectar of Devotion.* I thought of Prabhupāda in 1966 at one time today. I imagined I was telling someone about it. I spoke to this imaginary devotee like this: “You see, those were very simple days. We didn’t even call Prabhupāda by his title, Prabhupāda. We called him Swamijī. We had so much affection for him. Everything was very simple. Prabhupāda had no real followers then, just a few people coming forward. We were young men, not following any religious life at all. There is something very sweet and simple about it—those days in New York when Prabhupāda first came and reached out to us.”

## 4.6: December 1

Today was a big event in the Bombay temple—the inauguration of the Gita-Jayanti month. The temple has pledged to distribute one hundred thousand *Bhagavad-gītās* in one month. The mayor came, and various devotees spoke on the stage (including myself). The festival managers introduced me as an old devotee who was there with Prabhupāda in the beginning in New York City. I tried to approach the subject of the *Bhagavad-gītā* from that stance, telling incidents about book distribution in the early days. Prabhupāda would be pleased by the numbers of his books being distributed, and by how his temple was such a vital place that the mayor came to speak.

The atmosphere in the Bombay temple on a Sunday is social, not so much a deep, internal mood of worship. Yet all these nondevotees who think mainly of business, family, and sense gratification, come on Sundays and enthusiastically take *darśana* of the Deities: “Jaya Rādhā-Rasabihārī, Jaya Sīta-Rāma.””Afterward, they flock into the auditorium and pledge their money to distribute the *Gītās*. Prabhupāda is pleased to see these people acting religiously. As full-time devotees, we have to encourage everyone to serve Kṛṣṇa according to their capacity, and for this, we have to give credit to the ISKCON managers here in Bombay.

There was a real hotel banquet air to the evening. The devotees had set up a long table for the speakers with microphones and name tags. Free pens were handed out and special stamps and brochures. Everything was done very efficiently and on a high material standard for prosperous Bombay. But it was all connected to Kṛṣṇa and Prabhupāda. I didn’t feel very deeply involved, and I didn’t feel able to meditate deeply on Prabhupāda and Kṛṣṇa either, so I got out as soon as I could. I gave my lecture and instead of returning to my seat, I went upstairs and took *prasādam*.

## 4.6: December 2, ekādaśī

On *ekādaśī* in Bombay, it is traditional to read from the *Śrīla Prabhupāda-līlāmṛta* or the *Conversations* books. This morning we gathered in Prabhupāda’s room on the fifth floor of the tower and read from a 1967 conversation where Prabhupāda described scenes of Caitanya-*līlā* for a dramatic performance. He wanted Hayagrīva to write the play and then the devotees to act it out. Devotees asked me to read the section. As I did, I felt moved by it.

I also told this story in the biography. It was humorous because of the contrast between Hayagrīva and Śrīla Prabhupāda. Prabhupāda is obviously tasting the ecstasy of Lord Caitanya’s *līlā.* He was in the mood of the different *līlās,* sometimes very jolly and sometimes transcendentally sad, as when he described the disappearance of Haridāsa Ṭhākura. Hayagrīva was hearing these pastimes for the first time and obviously didn’t understand so much of what Prabhupāda was describing. For example, when Prabhupāda told him about Kṣircora Gopi-nātha, Hayagrīva thought it was Lord Caitanya as a *sannyāsī* who stole the condensed milk. He also had trouble pronouncing all the names.

I listened to a Prabhupāda lecture tape while I took *prasādam* today. This tape was from 1969. He had devotees reading aloud from the *Bhagavad-gītā,* and then he would interrupt them to comment. One devotee read from the purport that there is no void anywhere in God’s existence. Prabhupāda said that this was a fact. Wherever you go on land or air or sea, there will always be some form of life.

I think this is also true about Prabhupāda consciousness. There is no void of Prabhupāda’s presence for us in ISKCON. We’re never without Prabhupāda as long as we’re following some duties. Today, for example, I talked with a disciple about his service; later I spoke with a Godbrother who is on the GBC about how I can help him in his zone as a *sannyāsī*. All of these things are connected to Prabhupāda, even if specifically we were not recounting memories of him or repeating what he wrote in his books.

## 4.6: December 3

This morning, the devotee giving class gave a heavy book distribution lecture to enthuse us about the Prabhupāda marathon. Later in the day, I read this in the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta:* “Without accepting a bona fide spiritual master coming in disciplic succession, one cannot find out the real purpose of devotional service. Therefore, one has to accept the shelter of a bona fide spiritual master and agree to be directed by him. The first business of a pure devotee is to satisfy his spiritual master, whose only business is to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness” (*C.c. Madhya* 19.167, purport).

Prabhupāda’s statements about spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness make us wonder, “How will I do this?” Sometimes we may become turned off by devotees who stress preaching as the only service if they have a narrow-minded conception of what preaching is, yet we cannot avoid Prabhupāda’s emphasis on spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness. In this way Prabhupāda arrests us. He stops us and asks us what we are doing to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness. If we reply, “Nothing,” then we cannot expect Prabhupāda to be pleased. We cannot have favorable “meditations” without satisfying the spiritual master “whose only business is to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness.”

But we also have to recognize that that response within us may come in different ways. We have to begin preaching immediately or at least prepare ourselves to preach. We also have to develop our internal Kṛṣṇa consciousness along with external action.

I appreciate the way Prabhupāda stops us. He is right, of course. We don’t need to be guilty about our lackings, or fearful that we won’t be able to come up to Prabhupāda’s standards; we simply have to find a way to respond that works for us. Prabhupāda is not narrow-minded. He wants to evoke within us some kind of response so that, according to our capacity, we can “tell everyone we meet about Kṛṣṇa.” One other point: our response should be as simple and direct as we can make it.

## 4.6: December 4, New Delhi

Today was a day of distractions as my morning program was interrupted by airplane travel. Toward the afternoon, however, I managed to pick up Prabhupāda’s *Bhagavad-gītā As It Is* and look at it. I had a motive; someone suggested to me that I might be asked to lecture at the house where we are staying. But by Prabhupāda’s grace, I found more than just some lecture preparation when I read the book.

I was able to sense the great power Prabhupāda actually had to convert nondevotees into lovers of Kṛṣṇa. Even today there are many devotees of Kṛṣṇa (we might even say many pure devotees) living in holy places like Vraja. But these devotees mainly live within a small orbit of influence over others. One can philosophize and say that on the highest level, everything is already perfect and within Kṛṣṇa’s control. But the fact that billions of living entities are suffering without knowledge of Kṛṣṇa is reality. Therefore, all glory should be offered to the Lord’s mercy by which He grants release to those who are suffering in *saṁsāra.* All glories to the agent He uses for this purpose, our Śrīla Prabhupāda.

From Prabhupāda’s preface in *Bhagavad-gītā As It Is:*

The Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement is essential in human society … everyone should know how God, or Kṛṣṇa, is great, and everyone should know the factual position of the living entities.
