Everyone should know that a living entity is eternally a servant and that unless one serves Kṛṣṇa, one has to serve illusion in different varieties of the three modes of material nature and thus wander perpetually within the cycle of birth and death …

Prabhupāda wrote about this and then he came to America and persuaded us to take to Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

I also realized how many devotees have come to Kṛṣṇa consciousness by reading the *Bhagavad-gītā.* We had no intention of becoming devotees, but somehow Prabhupāda was able to get through to us. He was able to convince us in a nonsectarian way about the fact that we are suffering under the modes of nature. Kṛṣṇa is our only relief. It is this wonderful *śakti*, this compassion, the effectiveness of Prabhupāda’s preaching that made me feel deep appreciation as I read the *Gītā’s* preface. It made me want to tell others about Prabhupāda.

As for preparing for a lecture, I turned to the eighteenth chapter and plotted out some points to make from verses 18.67 to 18.71. One could say how the message of the *Bhagavad-gītā* should not be preached to people who are not austere. But people who are not envious can be told. The pure devotees hold open classes to tell others about Kṛṣṇa. Similarly, I want to tell people about Prabhupāda. But that means I have to myself be appreciative and realized and faithful. I am eternally his disciple.

Recognizing Prabhupāda’s compassion in his writings also gives deeper meaning to my new awareness that Prabhupāda has an identity beyond what I know. I can only pray to him to save me. I am glad to return to his standard *Bhagavad-gītā* teachings. Just to read his righteous indignation against those who misinterpret *Bhagavad-gītā,* his strong assertions that *Bhagavad-gītā* is for the whole world and that the whole world is in illusion, and his direct guidance to the lotus feet of Kṛṣṇa, are very relishable and nourishing. Prabhupāda is the great *ācārya* for introducing people to Kṛṣṇa consciousness. As he writes, “This knowledge constitutes a great science, and each and every living being has to hear for his own interest.” And Prabhupāda is also the *ācārya* who will take us past our initial faith to the higher stages of *niṣṭhā, bhāva,* and *premā.*

## 4.6: December 11, Vṛndāvana

Finally back in Vṛndāvana. This morning I went to Prabhupāda’s rooms. There was only one other person there, but she was sitting up straight right in front of Prabhupāda’s desk. I couldn’t see Prabhupāda, and neither could I go up close to him to offer obeisances.

Sometimes Prabhupāda seems to be blocked from our view by different things, even by another of his disciples. We can only be patient about it. There will always be another opportunity.

After the class this morning, when the lecturer asked for questions, I made a point which supported something he said. I gave an example from Prabhupāda. The lecturer had said that the Super-soul is with us in the material world, but when we go to Kṛṣṇaloka, He is no longer there. I gave a supporting example: Prabhupāda explains in a pur-port how at Kumbha-melā, a big city suddenly arises and a governor is appointed to see to its concerns. But after the Kumbha-melā, the city is dismantled and the governor goes back to his previous duties.

I then raised my hand a second time and said something about Prabhupāda teaching that devotees who practice Kṛṣṇa consciousness can get help from the Lord in the heart. The lecturer then asked me if I knew any particular examples of this. I said yes, Prabhupāda used to encourage the ISKCON artists, when they asked for directions on how to paint, to pray to the Lord in the heart. Prabhupāda would give some specific directions, but then he would say, “You are sincere. Kṛṣṇa will tell you in the heart how to do it.” The lecturer told a Prabhupāda story of his own.

Later in the day, when I had a little time and wanted to read *rasika* literature, I felt inclined to read something *rasika* that was presented by Prabhupāda. So I turned to some *Antya-līlā* sections of *Caitanya-caritāmṛta*. That is the ideal combination, the *mādhurya* pastimes as discussed by Prabhupāda in his purports.

## 4.6: December 12

It’s almost 7:00 p.m. I felt ill today, so I didn’t attend the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* class. I mention this because in scanning over the day’s activities, the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* class is the obvious point where I can get remembrances of Prabhupāda. Today I slept. Another obvious place is when I read. I did do some reading today, Prabhupāda’s *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* purports.

A devotee wrote me and asked why we say Kṛṣṇa is the source of Viṣṇu rather than the other way around. I wanted to find a quote to answer this letter with, so I referred to the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* section where Lord Caitanya teaches Vyenkata Bhaṭṭa. Once I found the reference, I read the whole section.

## 4.6: December 14

I’m going through a transition which I hope will deepen my appreciation for Prabhupāda. What does it mean to be in transition? If one can’t always turn to remember him in the ways that one is used to, in ways that one used to count on, and one hasn’t yet developed a more advanced understanding of Prabhupāda, then it can be said that one is in between stages. Being afraid or refusing to go forward is known as *niyamāgraha*. I am trying to go forward. At least that’s positive.

I gave *Bhāgavatam* class today. I was able to recall some Prabhupāda stories like the time he was on television in New York City so long ago. During the question and answer period, I suddenly remembered something about Prabhupāda. A devotee sparked the memory by asking, “Could you give some suggestions how an ISKCON manager can keep clear consciousness?” I started speaking about Prabhupāda’s routine and how he kept his schedule. I said Prabhupāda was a liberated soul, but he also acted like a very strict *sādhaka.* He kept the temple schedule and chanted *japa* every day. Then just by glancing at Prabhupāda on the *vyāsāsana,* I felt inspired to describe his daily schedule.

He took rest around 10 p.m. and got up around 1:00 a.m. to translate *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam.* After that he chanted *japa,* then went on a walk, then came back and gave the *Bhāgavatam* class. He did everything at specific times during the day. He didn’t easily stray from that schedule in the name of an emergency. *We* might do that, “Oh, I didn’t chant my rounds today. I didn’t do any reading,” but Prabhupāda rarely did. Prabhupāda’s disciples knew that he did more management than anyone, and yet they were astounded by how he could keep following the spiritual schedule. The atmosphere in Bombay when they were fighting for the land and consulting many lawyers was intense. Usually in the evening, the devotees would meet with Prabhupāda and discuss their strategy for the law case. Right in the midst of all these managerial discussions, Prabhupāda would look at his watch and tell them it was time for class and *kīrtana.* The devotees would think, “How could Prabhupāda possibly go to class and *kīrtana* in the midst of all these heavy events?” But he did.

## 4.6: December 16, 1:30 a.m.

A little Prabhupāda moment: Yesterday in the late afternoon, Madhu and I were walking on the *parikrama* trail. I told him of my desire to return to Vṛndāvana next year to teach a course in the VIHE. I have pretty much decided to teach on Prabhupāda’s life. When I said this, Madhu replied, “Yes everything always comes back to Prabhupāda.” If one studies *rāgānugā bhakti,* then one soon comes to the point of understanding the importance of worshiping, following, and pleasing the spiritual master. That is the foundation of *any* form of *bhakti.* Ultimately, one has to pray to Prabhupāda to know his eternal identity and to follow him to Vraja. Similarly, any direction one takes in writing or preaching or temple management has to be in relation to Prabhupāda, his person, his mission, the source.

Tonight I sat with my disciple Navadvīpa and prepared an outline for a course on Prabhupāda. I felt enthusiastic just by talking about it. Off the top of my head, I made a list of nineteen classes on the life of Prabhupāda. One of the main things I want to stress are the two sides of Prabhupāda: the *sādhaka* form as he was manifested in this world, and his form in *kṛṣṇa-līlā.* Another main theme is that everyone has a living relationship with Prabhupāda. It’s not that I want to set myself up as the main authority on Prabhupāda, but I want to invite devotees to discuss their own feelings and realizations. In that context, I will also give my own interpretation and impressions of Prabhupāda.

I have an idea of how this might work. We might be discussing why Prabhupāda left America in 1969, and someone might say, “I read the *Prabhupāda-līlāmṛta* and I don’t agree that Prabhupāda was driven from America by the misbehavior of his disciples. I think he thought it was time to go preach in India and that’s why he left.” But then another devotee could say, “Certainly Prabhupāda wanted to preach in India, but you can’t disregard the whole list of facts as to the difficulties that were going on in America. Prabhupāda himself wrote a letter and actually said he was going to India to get away from the offensive mentality of some of his disciples.” In this way, different opinions may surface in the class, and one opinion doesn’t have to hold sway over another opinion in the absolute sense.

## 4.6: December 18

I think today I felt some kind of resurgence of identity as Prabhupāda’s disciple. In other words, whatever advanced association or advanced know-ledge I have gained here in Vṛndāvana, I know I have to assimilate it within the context of being Prabhupāda’s disciple. I didn’t experience this so much in an intellectual way, but rather as a feeling of security. It probably had something to do with the fact that I am nearing the end of my stay in Vṛndāvana. I will soon be going back to my duties in the West, those duties that are part of my identity as Prabhupāda’s disciple, my writing and traveling and being with disciples. I think I have a better sense, after having been here, that my service *is* actually service to Prabhupāda. It is mainstream activity in ISKCON. Prabhupāda’s movement is not just an institutional structure; there is room for all of us to make a real contribution.

I now feel assured that I am not just serving in a faulty institution, but I am working in a movement that is enduring at heart. I feel more conviction that I *want* to follow my spiritual master by working in his movement. In other words, whatever discrepancies exist in ISKCON are just like so much debris floating on the holy Ganges.

The earth sometimes shifts deep under the ocean. These shifts are very important to the whole planet, but they usually aren’t noticed immediately. Or we can think of the ice shifting at the poles. A big ice sheet breaks away and starts to move to sea; the movement is so slow and imperceptible. I feel like I have experienced such an imperceptible shift in my feelings toward Prabhupāda. I think these feelings are coming within the context of my bigger, more long term Prabhupāda consciousness. This is not a revolutionary change, just a quiet shifting of my focus that will increase my devotion to Prabhupāda.

I was alone most of the day, reading in my room. I read the last chapter of the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta;* then I read a translation of Rūpa Gosvāmī’s *Stava-mālā*. I feel secure.

## 4.6: December 19

I ordered some pictures of myself with Prabhupāda from the Bhaktivedanta Archives. They may be ready for me when I go back to America. When I inquired, I heard that there were two hundred and fifty photos of Prabhupāda in which I also appear. I couldn’t afford all of them, so I asked for fifty in which there appeared to be some relationship or closeness between Prabhupāda and I. I hope to be able to carry a small album of these pictures wherever I travel, and that they will remind me that I actually walked beside him and was with him.

Memory is certainly a decaying faculty. One tends to forget the essence of things. We tend to forget what a great privilege it was to be with such a great spiritual teacher who was empowered to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness all over the world. A spiritual teacher who was so dear to Kṛṣṇa that he was given the *śakti* to write so many books and establish a worldwide movement. *Kṛṣṇa-śakti… . parvarta.* I pray the pictures will remind me.

Every day we meet people who were affected by Prabhupāda. Either they became his disciples, or they are now serving in his movement by serving his servants. There are others who never took initiation into ISKCON, but who have been serving Prabhupāda, remembering Prabhupāda, for decades. And there are others who have been deeply effected by reading his books. I heard that a senior God-brother is writing a collection of memories of his times with Prabhupāda. He is doing it in a creative way by stringing the stories together on a literary garland.

It’s a fact that Kṛṣṇa conscious teachings are my life work. I either speak the teachings or I write the teachings or I live my life according to them and try to realize their nature in myself. So the form of Kṛṣṇa conscious teachings which I live by is the form in which Prabhupāda presents it. I live my life according to his expressions and particular way of presenting the Kṛṣṇa conscious philosophy. Although I may now read translations of books by the Six Gosvāmīs, I feel safe with the foundation Prabhupāda gave me. I am trying to add more stories on the building he created in me. He has sheltered me with the *siddhānta.*

## 4.6: December 20

Today while walking on the *parikrama* trail in Vṛndāvana, I tried to drink it in a little more than usual because I realized I have only two more days left here. Thinking like this, I looked at the Rādhā-Madana-mohana temple domes ahead of me and then at other sights, even at nearby hogs which seemed to be a necessary part of the picture. When I turned and started walking in the opposite direction, I saw a peacock fly by. “Peacocks really belong in Vṛndāvana,” I thought, “and they are part of the same eternal Vṛndāvana that we hear about in the *rasika śāstras*.” Peacocks in the West in ISKCON country *āśramas* never seem like they belong there. They sit up in the trees like big fowl, but I just don’t think of Vṛndāvana when I see them.

Immediately, my mind went to Prabhupāda. Prabhupāda actually liked the peacocks at the Detroit Deva-sadan temple. He remarked with amusement that the peacocks used to eat the flowers and that was the only problem. So if Prabhupāda approves that Vṛndāvana can be exported to ISKCON temples in the West, why should I say that it’s not true? Prabhupāda wasn’t so conservative or so limited. He used to describe people who couldn’t appreciate the widespread preaching efforts of Kṛṣṇa consciousness as crippled-minded.

Thus this meditation while on the *parikrama* trail turned into a fuller appreciation of Prabhupāda. Certainly Prabhupāda tasted Vṛndāvana much more deeply than I do, but as he began the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement and saw to its spread around the world by repeatedly visiting his centers, he also encouraged the bits of Vṛndāvana he saw springing up in his Western temples. He even called little hills Govardhana Hill and small ponds New Rādhā-kuṇḍa. Prabhupāda was dynamic. Vṛndāvana wasn’t just a rural village in India to him, and neither was it a place that could only be appreciated if one went to India. All glories to Prabhupāda’s broad vision.

## 4.6: December 22

Riding in the van today, some devotees were talking about how they don’t feel so much excitement or ecstasy in doing service. We agree that we shouldn’t blame Prabhupāda for this, that it’s our own lacking. Then some of us remembered old days when it was so exciting to serve Prabhupāda. I shared a memory of a time when Prabhupāda was in Canada and he wanted me to go to the U.S. immigration office in Boston and find out why his papers were delayed. I dressed in my suit and tie and went there on Prabhupāda’s behalf. It was a simple task, but I felt ecstasy as I carried it out.

Bhūrijana then recalled the time he visited Prabhupāda on 26 Second Avenue. He was sent out to purchase something called “Tiger’s Milk” for Prabhupāda. He remembered how happy he had felt, wandering around St. Marks Place trying to buy it for the Swami.

I’m sitting in my room on my last night in Vṛndāvana. I didn’t go down and see Prabhupāda in his rooms enough. Although I see him every day in his *samādhi-mandira,* it’s not the same as going to see him in his residential rooms. But if I go now, there’s a good chance I’ll meet someone on the stairs and that person will want to see me. Then I’ll have to come back to the room and talk, and I won’t end up seeing Prabhupāda. It’s probably better for me to stay here in my room and think about Prabhupāda than to attempt to see him.

I want to thank Prabhupāda personally for taking the anxiety to build this Vṛndāvana Krishna-Balarama Mandir. It cost him so much. He had to push to get it done, push the devotees to think of cement and raising money, etc. Someone can criticize and say Prabhupāda should have paid more attention to *bhajana* or to *rāsa-līlā,* or that he should have been more in Vṛndāvana consciousness, but he knew he had to provide adequate shelter for his Western disciples. And he had to teach us carefully what thinking of Kṛṣṇa in Vṛndāvana meant and didn’t mean so that we wouldn’t become *sahajiyās*. Whatever Prabhupāda did is perfect and complete. We can still build on that structure to realize more fully what Vṛndāvana is, as Prabhupāda wants us to.

Another memory from today—a devotee told a story of when Prabhupāda first came to India and traveled with his disciples in a group from one home to another. When they arrived in one place, Acyutānanda asked Prabhupāda, “What will our program be here?” Prabhupāda replied, “Our program is to cry for Kṛṣṇa.”

I hope I can return to Vṛndāvana. I beg Prabhupāda and the *dhāma* to please forgive me for any *sādhu-nindā* I committed while I was here, or worse, *guru aparādha, nāma aparādha*. Please forgive me for all the *aparādhas* I probably did commit here in the *dhāma*. I wish to be able to come back under the auspices of Śrīmatī Rādhārāṇī and serve in Vṛn-dāvana-*dhāma*. All glories to Prabhupāda. Please forgive me. Please also set me right so that I can be a better servant for you and always be a part of your movement .

This is Prabhupāda’s home. The bell is ringing for *sundara-ārati.* It’s the same bell we hear about in some Prabhupāda stories. Prabhupāda had to push his disciples just to get that bell properly installed. He worked with such effort to get Vṛndāvana together, and although there may still be discrepancies, it’s going on. I thank the managers of the Vṛndāvana temple, and I thank the devotees for allowing me to come here, even though I don’t make such a big contribution. Prabhupāda, please allow me to come here for *bhajana* in the future, and more writing, more lecturing, more attempts to be a better example. I really do want to become qualified and yet not proud. I want to be humble and surrender to Kṛṣṇa. Only you can teach me this. Please let me read your books. Let me never neglect them.

A younger devotee was saying today how it takes many years to build up one’s Kṛṣṇa consciousness in different ways. When he said that, I thought of the twenty-five years I have spent as Prabhupāda’s devotee; something precious has been built up, and I don’t want to ruin it. I don’t want to ruin my *guru-niṣṭhā.* It’s all I have. So in the name of reaching further for a relationship with Kṛṣṇa, please protect me, Prabhupāda, because the real thing is *gurupādāśraya.* Even in the spiritual world, that *gurupādāśraya* continues. One should never tamper with it. Please let me always build on that, Śrīla Prabhupāda.

Godbrothers who are writing memoirs asked me for tips how to write. I gave them technical advice—how to write flashbacks, how to do narrations, how to structure their writing, how to express themselves. I was also thinking of something I didn’t tell them. From time to time in the midst of your story or memoir, you can break into prayers to Śrīla Prabhupāda and speak honestly and openly.

The lights have gone out again. My sitting in the dark is symbolic, it seems. *Oṁ ajñana timirandhasya jnananjana salakaya*. Light a candle. I can think of Prabhupāda even in this darkness and pray for the inner light of chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa in his service. I leave tomorrow, but let me do everything as service to Prabhupāda.
